- [ ] **`reflector-noretry-1`** — Reflector and Synthesizer LLM calls have no retry, unlike Planner and DeerFlow planner  
  - **Where:** `Engine/Reflector.swift:116 (bare llm.complete), Engine/Synthesizer.swift:93-104 (bare llm.stream); cf. Planner.swift:61 and DeerFlowEngine.swift:442 which wrap in RetryPolicy`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** Retry coverage is inconsistent across the pipeline stages. Planner wraps its completion in `retry.run(...)` (Planner.swift:61) and DeerFlow's planner uses `RetryPolicy.networkDefault.run` (DeerFlowEngine.swift:442). But the Reflector calls `try await llm.complete(req)` with no retry (Reflector.swift:116) and the Synthesizer drains `llm.stream(req)` with no retry wrapper (Synthesizer.swift:93). Only AnthropicClient/GeminiClient have internal transient-retry (per grep); OpenAI-compatible/others rely on the engine-level RetryPolicy that these two stages skip. A reflector parse failure is caught and degraded to an empty critique (Reflector.swift:129-134), but a transient network throw in the reflector propagates and the synthesis stage has no resilience at all on non-Anthropic providers.  
  - **Impact:** On a flaky network or a 5xx, the synthesis stage (the single most expensive, user-facing stage) fails the whole run for OpenAI/DeepSeek/MiniMax/Kimi/Qwen/local providers, even though Planner/DeerFlow-planner would have recovered. Inconsistent resilience by provider is surprising to users.  
  - **Fix:** Thread a RetryPolicy into Reflector and Synthesizer (matching Planner's constructor pattern) and wrap the completion/stream-start in it. At minimum wrap the Synthesizer's initial stream acquisition; for streaming, retry only before first token to avoid duplicate partial output.  
  - **Verify:** Core claim verified: Reflector calls bare `try await llm.complete(req)` (Reflector.swift:116, no retry wrapper) and Synthesizer drains bare `llm.stream(req)` (Synthesizer.swift:93, no retry), while Planner wraps in retry.run (Planner.swift:61, retry property defined :9/:20) and DeerFlow planner uses RetryPolicy.networkDefault.run (DeerFlowEngine.swift:442). Inconsistent retry coverage is real. ONE supporting detail is inaccurate: the finding says 'Only AnthropicClient/GeminiClient have internal transient-retry' but OpenAICompatibleClient also has internal transient-retry (lines 64-138, linear backoff). However those client retries are all pre-response-only (no retry once bytes arrive, per code comments), so a mid-synthesis stream drop is still unprotected. Central thesis holds; P2 robustness appropriate.  

#### Cross-cutting: Concurrency & Data Races

- [x] **`concurrency-budgetmeter-charge-discard`** — ToolContext.charge silently discards budget-overflow throw via try?, defeating the token cap mid-tool  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/WorkerAgent.swift:167 (charge: { tokens in try? await self.budget.chargeTokens(tokens) })`  
  - **Category:** correctness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** Tools charge tokens through `ToolContext.charge`, which wraps `budget.chargeTokens` in `try?`. `chargeTokens` throws `EngineFailure(.budgetExceeded)` once the cap is crossed, but `try?` discards it, so tokens consumed inside tool execution still increment `tokensUsed` yet never stop the worker. The cap is only re-checked at the top of the next `toolLoop` iteration via `budget.checkWallClock()` (wall-clock, not tokens) and the explicit `chargeTokens(completion.totalTokens)` after each LLM call. A tool that charges large token counts (e.g. KB embedding) can blow far past `maxTokens` before the loop notices.  
  - **Impact:** Token budget can overshoot the configured cap (fast=60k, standard=300k) by a tool's worth of tokens with no enforcement, undermining the user's cost ceiling. Not a data race, but a correctness gap in the budget invariant the meter is meant to guarantee.  
  - **Fix:** Have `charge` propagate the throw (or set a per-worker over-budget flag the loop checks). At minimum, after a tool call, re-check `try await budget.chargeTokens(0)` equivalent / a `try budget.ensureUnderToken()` and break the loop when over.  
  - **Verify:** WorkerAgent.swift:167 `charge: { tokens in try? await self.budget.chargeTokens(tokens) }`. BudgetMeter.chargeTokens (AgentBudget.swift:67-73) increments `tokensUsed` (line 68) then throws EngineFailure(.budgetExceeded) when over `maxTokens` (lines 69-72); the `try?` discards that throw. The ToolContext.charge type is `@Sendable (Int) async -> Void` (Tool.swift:18) — non-throwing by design, so a charging tool (WikipediaTool:139, WebReaderTool:69, KnowledgeBaseTool:65, PDFReaderTool:73, WebSearchTool:81, RedditTool:163) cannot abort the worker. The cap is re-enforced only at the next loop iteration's `try await budget.chargeTokens(completion.totalTokens)` (line 131, NOT try?) and `checkWallClock()` (line 125). So a tool charging a large count overruns maxTokens within the iteration before the next LLM call notices. Bounded but real; P2 appropriate.  
- [x] **`concurrency-livesession-forecastrun-no-deinit-cancel`** — LiveSession and ForecastRun never cancel their Tasks on deinit  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Interface/AppEnvironment.swift:321-499 (LiveSession, streamTask) and Swift Deep Research/Forecast/ForecastRun.swift:80-238 (pollTask)`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** Neither @MainActor @Observable controller has a `deinit` that cancels its long-running Task (`streamTask` / `pollTask`). Grep confirms zero `deinit` in the app's Swift tree. The tasks capture `[weak self]`, so the object can deallocate, but the Task itself (and the network request it drives) keeps running until it observes cancellation — which only happens via explicit `cancel()`/`detach()`. For `LiveSession`, `startResearch` calls `live?.cancel()` before replacing, and `detachForecast` handles `ForecastRun`, so the common paths are covered; but any code path that drops the last reference without calling cancel (e.g. an unexpected reassignment, or future refactor) leaks an in-flight engine run / 2s polling loop that keeps hitting the backend.  
  - **Impact:** Latent leak: a research stream or a forecast status-poll loop can keep executing and consuming network/CPU after its UI object is gone. Today it's contained by the explicit cancel calls, but it's a fragile invariant with no defense-in-depth.  
  - **Fix:** Add `deinit { streamTask?.cancel() }` to LiveSession and `deinit { pollTask?.cancel() }` to ForecastRun (deinit on a @MainActor type can call cancel synchronously since cancel is non-isolated-safe). This makes teardown self-healing regardless of call path.  
  - **Verify:** Grep confirms zero `deinit` in the app Swift tree (only matches are in logs/*.json edit records). LiveSession (AppEnvironment.swift:319-499) holds `streamTask` and ForecastRun (ForecastRun.swift:80) holds `pollTask`; neither has a deinit. Both Tasks capture `[weak self]` (runEngine line 466, ForecastRun start line 105 / pollLoop / resume line 150) so the object deallocates and ingestion short-circuits via `guard let self else { return }`, but the in-flight engine run / 2s polling loop (pollLoop line 195-238 sleeps 2s) keeps running and hitting the backend until it observes cancellation. Common paths DO cancel: live?.cancel() (AppEnvironment 278,296), detachForecast() before reassigning forecast (101,120,139; detachForecast at 223-230 cancels/detaches). Residual is a future-refactor / unexpected-reassignment leak. A leaked poll loop hits the backend every 2s and a leaked engine run burns tokens — non-trivial cost. P2 stands as a defense-in-depth gap.  
- [ ] **`concurrency-unbounded-event-stream-mainactor`** — Engine event AsyncThrowingStream uses .unbounded buffering; all events drained and applied on MainActor  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/ResearchEngine.swift:47-61 (bufferingPolicy: .unbounded, emit closure) and Swift Deep Research/Interface/AppEnvironment.swift:466-562 (LiveSession.ingest on @MainActor)`  
  - **Category:** bottleneck · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** The orchestrator stream is created with `bufferingPolicy: .unbounded`, and the `emit` @Sendable closure is invoked synchronously from every worker actor and the synthesis token loop. The sole consumer is `LiveSession.runEngine`'s `for try await event` loop running on `@MainActor`, where `ingest(event)` does non-trivial work per event: JSON-encodes an `EventEnvelope` and writes it to SwiftData (`store.appendEvent`), plus array appends. With 6 thorough-mode workers emitting `reasoningDelta`/`toolInvoked`/`toolResult` and synthesis emitting `tokenDelta` per token, the producer can outrun the MainActor consumer; .unbounded means the buffer grows without backpressure and every token's SwiftData write executes on the main thread.  
  - **Impact:** On thorough runs with fast providers (or local Ollama bursts), UI can stutter and memory can spike — each `tokenDelta` triggers a synchronous JSON encode + SwiftData append on the MainActor, and the unbounded buffer retains all of it if the UI thread is busy. This is the most likely source of perceptible jank during synthesis.  
  - **Fix:** Either move per-event SwiftData persistence off the MainActor (batch/coalesce writes on a background ModelActor), or switch to `.bufferingNewest(n)` for high-frequency `tokenDelta`/`reasoningDelta` events so a slow UI drops intermediate deltas instead of accumulating them. Coalesce `tokenDelta` appends (you already cap `tokenStream` at 4000) before the DB write.  
  - **Verify:** ResearchEngine.swift:47 creates the stream with `bufferingPolicy: .unbounded`; the @Sendable emit closure (lines 59-61) is a thin `continuation.yield` invoked synchronously from parallel workers (TaskGroup at line 258, group.addTask at 272, worker emits at WorkerAgent 52/135/151/181/207) and the synthesis token loop. Sole consumer is LiveSession.runEngine's `for try await event` on @MainActor (AppEnvironment.swift:466-471); ingest() (501-562) JSON-encodes an EventEnvelope and calls `store.appendEvent` per event, and appendEvent (ResearchStore.swift:165-180) does `context.insert` + `context.save()` — a SwiftData write. ResearchStore is @MainActor (line 6). So every token-delta during synthesis drives a main-thread SwiftData save with no backpressure. Accurate; P2 reasonable.  
- [x] **`concurrency-webreader-continuation-leak`** — HiddenWebView continuation can leak or double-resume across WKNavigationDelegate callbacks  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/ResearchTools/WebReaderTool.swift:154-182 (HiddenWebView.load / webView delegate callbacks)`  
  - **Category:** concurrency · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** adjusted  
  - **Problem:** `load(_:)` stores a `CheckedContinuation<Void, Error>` in `self.continuation` and relies on exactly one of `didFinish` / `didFail` / `didFailProvisionalNavigation` to fire to resume it. The guard is only `continuation?.resume(); continuation = nil`. Two real hazards exist: (a) WKWebView can deliver a provisional failure followed by a separate failure, or a redirect that yields a `didFinish` after an error — the `nil`-after-first-resume guards a *single* object instance, but if a *second* `load()` is started while the first is in flight (it isn't here, but the JS-eval path awaits `load` then `Task.sleep(750ms)` then `evalString` without re-entrancy protection), the new continuation overwrites the old one, orphaning the first await forever. (b) If the WKWebView is torn down (host deallocated, or the consuming Task is cancelled during the 30s `timeoutInterval`) without any delegate callback firing, the continuation is never resumed and the awaiting worker hangs until the surrounding budget wall-clock cap fires. There is no `stop()`/cancel path that resumes the pending continuation.  
  - **Impact:** A worker can hang on a single JavaScript-fallback page fetch until the per-run wall-clock budget (180-1800s) expires, wasting the entire remaining budget on one stuck source. CheckedContinuation will also trap (crash) in debug builds if a delegate path ever double-resumes, e.g. a provisional failure then a hard failure.  
  - **Fix:** Resume-exactly-once defensively: capture the continuation into a local, set `self.continuation = nil` BEFORE resuming, and resume the local. Add cancellation handling via `withTaskCancellationHandler` (or resume with a CancellationError in a `stop()` that the worker calls), so a cancelled/torn-down load doesn't orphan the await. Consider giving `load` its own timeout that resumes-throwing if no delegate callback arrives.  
  - **Verify:** WebReaderTool.swift:156-182. The 'double-resume' is actually prevented: each delegate callback resumes then sets `continuation = nil` (lines 174-175, 178, 181) and WKWebView delivers exactly one of didFinish/didFail per navigation. The 're-entrant second load overwrites continuation' sub-claim is explicitly hypothetical — the finding itself says 'it isn't here'; extractJavaScript (line 199-215) creates a fresh HiddenWebView per call (line 200) and calls load() exactly once, and evalString uses the separate `webView.evaluateJavaScript` async API (line 189), not the continuation. The genuine residual gap: `withCheckedThrowingContinuation` (line 166) has NO cancellation handler and there is no stop()-on-cancel path, so if the consuming worker Task is cancelled mid-load the continuation is not resumed by the cancellation — the await waits for a WKWebView delegate callback. The 30s `timeoutInterval` (line 168) bounds a network stall (it yields didFailProvisionalNavigation), but a pure cancel produces no delegate callback, so a cancelled fetch hangs up to ~30s before WKWebView times out. There is no external timeout/TaskGroup wrapper (grep confirmed). Real robustness gap but bounded by the 30s timeout, not an unbounded leak or double-resume — hence P2 not P1.  

#### Cross-cutting: Security & Process/Network Safety

- [x] **`sec-custom-endpoint-cleartext-5`** — Custom LLM endpoint accepts any scheme — Bearer API key sent over cleartext http  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Interface/SettingsSheet.swift:767-774 (commitCustomHost); LLM/OpenAICompatibleClient.swift:79-80,261-262`  
  - **Category:** security · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** commitCustomHost only checks `url.scheme != nil`, accepting http://, ftp://, or even file:// as the custom OpenAI-compatible base URL. OpenAICompatibleClient then sends `Authorization: Bearer <apiKey>` to whatever host resolves. Unlike commitHost (seekdb, line 1207) and commitQwenHost (line 804) which restrict to http/https and require a host, the custom-endpoint path has the weakest validation despite carrying a real API key. A typo or attacker-influenced setting could exfiltrate the key in cleartext over http to an arbitrary host.  
  - **Impact:** API keys can be transmitted unencrypted to an arbitrary/typo'd endpoint, enabling interception or accidental leakage.  
  - **Fix:** Mirror the seekdb/Qwen validation: require scheme in {http,https} and a non-nil host; warn (or block) when a key is configured but the scheme is http and the host is not loopback. Consider refusing to attach the Bearer header to plain-http non-loopback hosts.  
  - **Verify:** commitCustomHost (SettingsSheet.swift:767-774) only checks `url.scheme != nil`, accepting http://, ftp://, file://. commitHost/seekdb (line 1206-1218) and commitQwenHost (804-810) both require scheme==http/https AND url.host!=nil — the custom path is genuinely the weakest. ProviderRegistry.swift:199-206 builds OpenAICompatibleClient for .custom with the .custom Keychain key and the unvalidated baseURL; OpenAICompatibleClient sends `Authorization: Bearer <apiKey>` (line 79-80, and 261-262 for listModels) to whatever host resolves, so a http:// custom endpoint leaks the key in cleartext. Minor inaccuracy in the finding: commitLMStudioHost (line 762) ALSO only checks `scheme != nil`, so it isn't a stricter baseline — but this does not change the verdict that the custom path under-validates while carrying a real key. P2 fair.  
- [ ] **`sec-keychain-accessibility-4`** — Keychain items use AfterFirstUnlock with no access control or device-only flag  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Shared/KeychainStore.swift:39-50`  
  - **Category:** security · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** set() stores keys with kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlock and no kSecAttrAccessControl. This means: (1) keys remain readable while the device is locked (any time after the first post-boot unlock), and (2) without ...ThisDeviceOnly, the item is eligible for iCloud Keychain sync / migration to other devices in backups. There is also no kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain and no access-control requiring user presence. For a credential store holding paid-API and Zep keys, AfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly is the appropriate minimum.  
  - **Impact:** Secrets are more broadly accessible than necessary (readable while locked, syncable off-device), widening the window and surface for credential theft.  
  - **Fix:** Use kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly (keys never need to leave this Mac). Consider adding kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain: true for the modern data-protection keychain. If any key warrants stronger protection, attach a kSecAttrAccessControl. Note the SecItemAdd at line 47 passes the accessibility only in the add path — ensure updates re-apply it.  
  - **Verify:** KeychainStore.set (lines 39-50) uses kSecAttrAccessibleAfterFirstUnlock with no kSecAttrAccessControl, no ...ThisDeviceOnly, and no kSecUseDataProtectionKeychain. The 'readable after first unlock' and 'eligible for backup/migration to other devices' concerns are valid, and AfterFirstUnlockThisDeviceOnly is the right minimum for a paid-API/Zep credential store. One nuance: the 'iCloud Keychain sync' claim is overstated — on Apple platforms sync requires kSecAttrSynchronizable=true (absent here), so items are not iCloud-synced; ThisDeviceOnly primarily blocks restore-to-other-device via encrypted backups/Migration Assistant. Core hardening gap and recommendation stand. P2 appropriate.  
- [ ] **`sec-local-backends-unauth-8`** — Local sidecar/MiroFish backends are unauthenticated and bound via http on a fixed port  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Knowledge/SeekDBClient.swift:24,125-136; Forecast/MiroFishClient.swift:28,230-260; Knowledge/SidecarSupervisor.swift:287-289`  
  - **Category:** security · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** The pyseekdb sidecar (127.0.0.1:9100) and MiroFish backend (127.0.0.1:5001) are spawned and called with no authentication token — any local process can hit /api/research/run (spends LLM tokens), upsert/query/reset the knowledge base (SeekDBClient.reset wipes all documents, line 60), or read dossiers. The Swift clients send no Authorization header to these local services. Because they are HTTP on loopback, any other user app or browser tab (via DNS-rebinding to 127.0.0.1, since there is no Origin/Host check evident on the Swift side) could drive them. SeekDBClient.reset() and MiroFish run/cancel are state-changing and destructive.  
  - **Impact:** Any local process (or a malicious web page via DNS rebinding) can issue destructive/costly commands to the knowledge base and forecast backend without credentials.  
  - **Fix:** Have the supervisor generate a per-launch random bearer token, pass it to the child via environment, require it on every request (Swift clients attach it), and have the backends reject requests lacking it. Additionally validate the Host header server-side (reject non-loopback Host) to blunt DNS rebinding. At minimum bind explicitly to 127.0.0.1 (not 0.0.0.0) — verify the spawned servers do.  
  - **Verify:** SeekDBClient (Knowledge/SeekDBClient.swift:24 default http://127.0.0.1:9100; request() at 125-159 sets only Accept/Content-Type, no Authorization) exposes destructive reset() POST /reset (line 60-62) and upsert/delete/query. MiroFishClient (Forecast/MiroFishClient.swift:28 default http://127.0.0.1:5001) calls /api/research/run (line 79, spends LLM tokens), cancel (104-107), setProvider (48) with no auth header (grep for Authorization/Bearer/token returns nothing). Any local process can drive both. The DNS-rebinding sub-claim depends on whether the Python servers validate Origin/Host (not visible in Swift), so that specific vector is uncertain — but the core 'unauthenticated, loopback http, state-changing/destructive endpoints' is fully confirmed on the Swift side. P2 fair.  
- [ ] **`sec-subprocess-config-path-6`** — Subprocesses execute scripts/interpreters from user-configurable paths (code-execution-by-config)  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Forecast/MiroFishSupervisor.swift:190-240 (setupScriptStream runs /bin/bash <repoRoot>/setup.sh), :422-461 (launch run.py), :397-418 (resolveInterpreter); Knowledge/SidecarSupervisor.swift:274-301`  
  - **Category:** security · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** MiroFishSupervisor runs `/bin/bash <repoRoot>/setup.sh` and `<repoRoot>/backend/.venv/bin/python run.py` where repoRoot comes from ForecastConfiguration.repoRootPath (a UserDefaults/Settings string, default ~/Downloads/mirofish/...). resolveInterpreter will execute whatever sits at `<repoRoot>/backend/.venv/bin/python`. To its credit the code uses Process with an argument array (no shell string interpolation, so there is no classic command injection), and PATH is augmented but not attacker-derived. However, pointing the app at any directory causes it to execute that directory's setup.sh / venv python — a Downloads-resident, attacker-droppable folder becomes auto-run code. The child also inherits the full parent environment (ProcessInfo.processInfo.environment), including any in-memory secrets.  
  - **Impact:** If a user is socially engineered to point Forecast at a malicious folder (or a Downloads folder is poisoned), the app will execute attacker-controlled bash/python with the user's privileges and inherited environment.  
  - **Fix:** Validate repoRoot before executing: require it to be a real directory, not under a writable-by-others location, and that setup.sh/run.py are owned by the current user and not group/world-writable (check stat ownership/perms). Pass a minimal, curated environment to children instead of inheriting the full process environment. Consider showing an explicit confirmation before first-time setup.sh execution from a new path.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed for MiroFishSupervisor: setupScriptStream runs `/bin/bash <repoRoot>/setup.sh` (lines 198-199), launch runs `<repoRoot>/backend/.venv/bin/python run.py` via resolveInterpreter (397-418/422-439), and repoRoot derives from ForecastConfiguration.repoRootPath, a persisted Settings string defaulting to ~/Downloads/mirofish/... — so pointing the app at an attacker-droppable Downloads folder auto-runs its setup.sh/venv python. The finding's own caveats are accurate: Process uses an argument array (no shell interpolation → no classic command injection), PATH is augmented from fixed dirs. Child inherits full parent env (childEnvironment line 464 / setupScriptStream line 201 both `ProcessInfo.processInfo.environment`). One citation inaccuracy: SidecarSupervisor.swift:274-301 is NOT config-driven — its script comes from Bundle.main resources / project tree (locateScript, line 506) and python from fixed system candidate paths (pythonCandidates, ~line 151), not from any user-configurable root. The MiroFish portion fully supports the finding; P2 stands.  

### P3 — Low / Polish (69)


#### Core Research Engine (native flow)

- [ ] **`engine-cache-not-used-for-cross-turn`** — ConversationContext.seenURLs / accumulatedSources are never wired into the SourceCache or worker prompts  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/ConversationContext.swift:69-72; ResearchEngine.swift:47-119`  
  - **Category:** improvement · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** ConversationContext exposes seenURLs (the URLs already fetched in prior turns) and accumulatedSources, and the doc comments say follow-up turns 'reuse the accumulated sources instead of starting from scratch.' But run() creates a fresh empty SourceCache() per run (ResearchEngine.swift:51) and never pre-populates it from context.accumulatedSources, and workers are never told about seenURLs. Only the planner gets a compact preamble (plannerPreamble). So a follow-up turn re-fetches every URL it needs again.  
  - **Impact:** Follow-up turns pay full fetch cost (latency, tokens, network) for URLs already retrieved in the same session — the cross-turn reuse the design promises does not happen at the fetch layer. Misses an easy capability/perf win for multi-turn sessions.  
  - **Fix:** Pre-seed the SourceCache with context.accumulatedSources at run start (store them under normalized keys) and pass seenURLs into the worker system prompt ('these URLs are already known: ...') so workers prefer new sources. This realizes the documented cross-turn behavior.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed. ConversationContext exposes seenURLs (ConversationContext.swift:70-72) and accumulatedSources, with the type doc-comment promising follow-up turns 'reuse the accumulated sources instead of starting from scratch.' But run() creates a fresh empty SourceCache() per run (ResearchEngine.swift:51) and never pre-populates from context.accumulatedSources; workers are constructed (lines 261-271) with that empty cache and are never told seenURLs (the worker system prompt, WorkerAgent.swift:69-97, has no seen-URL list). Only the planner gets context via plannerPreamble (Planner.swift:52 / ConversationContext.swift:50-67) which lists known domains but not the actual cached source bodies. So a follow-up turn re-fetches URLs it already has. Confirmed improvement opportunity; P3 fits (efficiency, not correctness).  
- [x] **`engine-double-error-and-throw`** — Error path both emits an .error event and finishes the stream throwing — UI shows the failure twice / inconsistently  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/ResearchEngine.swift:222-234; Interface/AppEnvironment.swift:468-491, 602-606`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** adjusted  
  - **Problem:** On a non-cancellation EngineFailure/unknown error the engine calls emit(.error(...)) AND continuation.finish(throwing:) (lines 226-227, 232-233). The consumer (AppEnvironment.swift:468) first ingests the .error event (sets failure, status=.failed, appends an error activity at line 602-605), then the for-try-await rethrows so the catch block (line 484-491) ALSO sets failure and status=.failed. Meanwhile the cancellation branch (line 223-224) emits .error then finish() WITHOUT throwing, so a user-initiated cancel is surfaced as a hard .error('Research cancelled') rather than a clean cancelled state — but AppEnvironment.cancel() already set status=.cancelled, which the ingested .error then overwrites to .failed.  
  - **Impact:** Inconsistent terminal state: a normal user cancel can be reported to the user as a failure (status flips .cancelled -> .failed and an error row is logged), and hard errors are double-recorded. Confusing UX and duplicate persisted error events (ingest persists every event, AppEnvironment.swift:502-510).  
  - **Fix:** Pick one error channel: either emit .error and finish() cleanly (don't also finish(throwing:)), letting the UI's event handler own terminal state; or finish(throwing:) and let the catch own it (drop the emit). For cancellation, prefer continuation.finish() with no .error event (or a dedicated .cancelled event) so the UI keeps the .cancelled status.  
  - **Verify:** Mostly confirmed but 'UI shows the failure twice' is overstated. Non-cancel path: engine calls emit(.error(failure)) AND continuation.finish(throwing: failure) (ResearchEngine.swift:226-227, 232-233). Consumer ingests .error (AppEnvironment.swift:602-605: sets failure, status=.failed, appends ONE error activity), then the for-try-await rethrows and the catch (line 484-491) sets failure+status=.failed AGAIN — but those setters are idempotent and the catch appends NO activity, so the user sees one error entry, not two. The genuinely real bug is the cancel race: engine emits .error(kind:.cancelled) then finish() without throwing (line 223-224); cancel() already set status=.cancelled (AppEnvironment.swift:498), and if that buffered .error is still ingested it overwrites status back to .failed (line 604) and surfaces a hard 'Research cancelled' error rather than a clean cancelled state. The double-set is harmless; the cancel-overwrite is a real but cosmetic state-consistency bug. P3 is the accurate severity, not P2.  
- [x] **`engine-hops-cap-decoupled-from-budget`** — maxHops=32 can exceed the per-worker tool-call cap silently, wasting LLM round-trips with no tool work  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/WorkerAgent.swift:107-159`  
  - **Category:** bottleneck · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** maxHops is a fixed 32 while maxToolCallsPerWorker is 6/20/36 per budget. In fast mode (6 tool calls) a misbehaving LLM that keeps emitting tool calls trips registerToolCall and breaks (line 156-159) — fine. But an LLM that keeps producing text+toolCalls where tool calls are cheap/duplicated can still burn up to 32 full LLM completions (each charged tokens) before maxHops stops it, and each completion is a serial network round-trip. The comment says maxHops is a 'safety net' but it is 5x larger than the fast-mode tool budget.  
  - **Impact:** In fast mode a single stuck worker can issue up to ~32 serial LLM calls (latency + untracked-vs-tracked token cost) before the hop cap fires, undermining the fast budget's intent. Serial round-trips dominate worker latency.  
  - **Fix:** Tie maxHops to the budget (e.g. maxToolCallsPerWorker + small slack) so the hop ceiling scales with mode, and break the loop once consecutive hops produce no new sources or repeat identical tool calls.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed. maxHops is a fixed 32 (WorkerAgent.swift:112) while maxToolCallsPerWorker is 6/20/36 (AgentBudget.swift fast=6, standard=20, thorough=36 — note the finding said '6/20/36', matching). registerToolCall trips and breaks the loop (line 156-159) when tool calls exceed the budget cap, so an LLM emitting many tool calls is stopped by the tool budget. But the loop charges tokens on every completion (line 131); an LLM that keeps emitting text + cheap/duplicate tool calls (or text-only with no tool calls breaks immediately at line 138-139, so the real exposure is text+toolcall pairs that pass the tool cap slowly) could in principle run up to 32 serial LLM completions before maxHops stops it. In fast mode the 6-tool-call cap halts it well before 32, so the realistic worst case is bounded by the tool cap in most modes; maxHops only dominates if tool calls stay under cap. Real but minor bottleneck; P3 correct.  
- [ ] **`engine-no-budget-charge-on-failed-llm`** — chargeTokens runs only on success; failed-but-billed provider calls leak budget; charge-before-use ordering can over/under count  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/WorkerAgent.swift:130-131; Synthesizer.swift:99-100`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** In the worker loop, budget.chargeTokens(completion.totalTokens) is called only after a successful complete() (WorkerAgent.swift:131). If a provider call partially streams (billable) then throws, no tokens are charged. Conversely tokens are charged after the full completion returns, so within a single hop the wall-clock/token caps can't stop an oversized call. The synthesizer charges on .usage chunks (Synthesizer.swift:100) which many providers only emit at stream end, so a long synthesis is fully generated (and billed) before any charge registers.  
  - **Impact:** Budget accounting drifts from real spend on failures and on providers that report usage only at completion — the token meter trails reality, so the cap fires late or not at all for the call that actually exceeded it. Combined with engine-token-budget-undercount, the effective cap is soft.  
  - **Fix:** Charge in a defer/finally around each LLM call using whatever partial usage is available, and check the cap before issuing the next call rather than relying on post-hoc charging to interrupt the current one.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed. Worker: budget.chargeTokens(completion.totalTokens) runs only after a successful llm.complete() returns (WorkerAgent.swift:130-131); if a provider partially streams (billable) then throws, the throw is caught at line 196-204 and no tokens are charged. Because the charge is post-completion, an oversized single call cannot be stopped mid-flight by the token cap (same root issue as the wall-clock finding). Synthesizer charges on .usage chunks (Synthesizer.swift:99-100); confirmed via LLMClient.complete (LLMClient.swift:63-64) that usage is captured from .usage chunks which many providers emit only at stream end, so a long synthesis is fully generated/billed before any charge registers. All claims verified against code. P3 is right — under/over-count edge cases on the unhappy path, not a primary-path defect.  
- [ ] **`engine-round-counting-mismatch`** — Round counter only advances when new subtasks survive dedup, so emitted iteration count diverges from reflection work done  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/ResearchEngine.swift:135-214`  
  - **Category:** correctness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** The loop is `while round < maxRounds` but `round += 1` happens only after newSubtasks pass the dedup filter (line 181). A reflection that produces no surviving subtasks breaks (line 177) without incrementing round, and reflectionStarted is emitted with the pre-increment round (line 148) while iterationStarted uses the post-increment round (line 183). The UI's currentRound/maxRounds (AppEnvironment.swift:514-515) can therefore show 'Reflecting (round 2)' followed by 'Iteration 3 of 6', and a run that does 5 reflector calls but only 2 productive rounds reports inconsistent progress.  
  - **Impact:** Progress UI is misleading; round numbers shown to the user don't correspond 1:1 to either reflection passes or synthesis passes. Harder to reason about how much work actually happened.  
  - **Fix:** Track a separate reflectionPass counter (incremented every iteration of the while loop) for reflectionStarted, and a researchRound counter for iterationStarted/plan growth. Emit consistent, monotonic numbers for each.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed (the specific example numbers are off-by-one but the structural claim holds). Loop is `while round < maxRounds` (ResearchEngine.swift:137) with round starting at 1; round += 1 happens only after dedup at line 181. reflectionStarted is emitted with the pre-increment round (line 148), iterationStarted with the post-increment round (line 183). So the first reflection emits reflectionStarted(round:1) then iterationStarted(round:2) — UI shows 'Reflecting (round 1)' then 'Iteration 2 of 6' (the finding wrote round 2/iteration 3, an off-by-one in the example, but the divergence is real). A reflection producing no surviving subtasks breaks at line 174-178 without incrementing round, so emitted iteration count diverges from reflector calls performed. Cosmetic progress-display inconsistency; P3 correct.  
- [ ] **`engine-unbounded-stream-buffer-leak`** — Unbounded event stream + per-token emission can accumulate large memory if the consumer stalls  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/ResearchEngine.swift:47, 59-61; Synthesizer.swift:96-98`  
  - **Category:** bottleneck · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** The AsyncThrowingStream is created with bufferingPolicy: .unbounded (line 47) and the synthesizer emits one .tokenDelta per chunk (Synthesizer.swift:98), plus every worker emits .reasoningDelta per completion and per tool result. The consumer is @MainActor (AppEnvironment.swift:466) doing non-trivial work per event (SwiftData persistence at AppEnvironment.swift:502-510, array appends, store writes). If the MainActor is busy, the producer (running off-MainActor) keeps yielding into an unbounded buffer with no backpressure.  
  - **Impact:** Under MainActor contention during heavy streaming the unbounded buffer grows without limit (every token + every persisted event), risking a memory spike on long thorough runs. No data loss, but a latent OOM/pressure vector and the per-event SwiftData write on MainActor is itself a throughput bottleneck.  
  - **Fix:** Consider .bufferingNewest(N) for a sane backpressure ceiling on high-frequency deltas, and/or coalesce token deltas (flush every K ms) before persisting. Move per-event SwiftData persistence off the MainActor or batch it so the consumer drains faster.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed as a low-severity backpressure concern. Stream created with bufferingPolicy: .unbounded (ResearchEngine.swift:47). Synthesizer emits one .tokenDelta per text chunk (Synthesizer.swift:96-98) and workers emit .reasoningDelta per completion (WorkerAgent.swift:135) plus .toolInvoked/.toolResult per call. Consumer runs on @MainActor (AppEnvironment.swift:466) and does non-trivial per-event work: SwiftData appendEvent (lines 502-510), upsertSource, array appends. If MainActor stalls, the off-MainActor producer keeps yielding into an unbounded buffer with no backpressure. Mitigations exist downstream (tokenStream trimmed to 4000 at line 562, activityLog to 500 at line 611) but those are post-ingest; the AsyncThrowingStream buffer itself is unbounded. Realistically bounded by run length and rare MainActor stalls, so P3 is correct.  
- [x] **`engine-workerid-collision-risk`** — WorkerID.make uses hashValue mod 0xFFFF plus a 6-char UUID prefix — low but nonzero collision risk across rounds  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Domain/ResearchEvent.swift:70-77`  
  - **Category:** correctness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** WorkerID.make builds 'w-<hash mod 65535>-<uuid.prefix(6)>'. Swift's String.hashValue is per-process-randomized (fine within a run) but is reduced mod 65535, and the UUID prefix is only 6 hex chars (~16.7M space). Across a thorough run that spawns many workers over 6 rounds, the UI keys WorkerSnapshot by WorkerID (AppEnvironment.swift:525) — a collision would overwrite one worker's live state with another's. The dedup in ResearchEngine also keys cache/source state by worker.  
  - **Impact:** Low probability, but a collision silently merges two workers in the UI (lost progress, wrong tool history) and in BudgetMeter.toolCallsByWorker (one worker's cap eaten by another). Not catastrophic but a latent data-integrity bug.  
  - **Fix:** Use a full UUID (or a monotonic counter) for the WorkerID rather than a truncated hash+prefix. The human-readable question hash can stay as a display label, but uniqueness should come from a full UUID.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed as described. WorkerID.make (ResearchEvent.swift:74-76) builds 'w-\(task.hashValue.magnitude % 0xFFFF)-\(UUID().uuidString.prefix(6))'. The hash component is reduced mod 65535 and the UUID prefix is 6 hex chars (~16.8M space). AppEnvironment keys WorkerSnapshot by WorkerID in a dictionary (AppEnvironment.swift:525 `workers[d.id] = ...`), so a collision would overwrite one worker's live snapshot. Probability is genuinely low across a 6-round run (a handful of workers), so P3 (low but nonzero, low impact = UI state mix-up not data loss) is correct. Note the 6-char UUID prefix makes a true collision astronomically unlikely; the realistic concern is the mod-65535 hash, but the UUID suffix saves it. Confirmed as a low-severity latent risk.  

#### DeerFlow Plan-and-Execute Engine

- [x] **`deerflow-10`** — knowledgeBaseDocTitles fetched but only threaded into the planner on iteration 1's prompt path; KB listing failure is silent  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:274-278, 102-113, 391-398`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** backgroundInvestigation lists KB documents via `try? await kbClient.listDocuments()` (line 276) and discards any error silently (the `try?`). The titles are passed to plan() on every iteration (line 108), but the kbNote is built unconditionally from them (lines 391-398) regardless of isReplan, so on re-plan iterations the planner is re-told the full KB doc list even though it already planned against it — minor prompt bloat. More notably, if listDocuments fails but the KB actually has docs, kbDocTitles is empty and the planner is never told the knowledge_base tool has content to search, weakening RAG-resource precedence (the very DeerFlow trait the doc comment at line 273 highlights).  
  - **Impact:** Transient SeekDB list failures silently demote the knowledge base from a first-class planner resource to an undiscoverable tool, so the planner may not instruct steps to query private docs first. Re-plan prompts also carry redundant KB-doc text.  
  - **Fix:** Fall back to a generic 'a private knowledge base is available; instruct steps to query knowledge_base first' note when listDocuments fails but useKnowledgeBase is true, and gate the verbose kbNote doc-list to the first planner call only (pass it empty on re-plan).  
  - **Verify:** Both sub-claims verified. listDocuments() is called with `try?` (DeerFlowEngine.swift:276) so any error is silently swallowed, leaving kbDocTitles empty; if the KB has docs but the list call fails, the planner is never told the knowledge_base tool has searchable content (kbNote at lines 391-398 is built only from non-empty knowledgeBaseDocs), weakening the RAG-resources precedence the doc comment at line 273 calls out. Also confirmed kbNote is built unconditionally from knowledgeBaseDocs regardless of isReplan, and titles are passed to plan() every iteration (line 108), so re-plan prompts re-include the full KB doc list — minor prompt bloat. Minor robustness; P3 correct.  
- [x] **`deerflow-11`** — Investigation pseudo-worker bypasses the per-worker tool-call budget, so its tool calls are uncapped and uncounted  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:260-312`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** backgroundInvestigation builds its own ToolContext (lines 260-267) and calls knowledge_base and web_search directly via tool.call(...), never going through BudgetMeter.registerToolCall (which is the cap enforced for real workers in WorkerAgent.swift:156). It charges tokens through the context.charge closure, so token budget is honored, but the per-worker tool-call ceiling is not applied to the investigation. This is benign today (it makes at most 2 calls) but is an unguarded path: if the investigation is ever extended to iterate or fetch URLs, nothing bounds it.  
  - **Impact:** Currently low (fixed 2 calls), but it is an inconsistency: the investigation is exempt from the tool-call governance every other agent obeys, and the BudgetMeter's toolCallsByWorker map will not include the investigation worker, so any future per-worker accounting/reporting under-counts it.  
  - **Fix:** Route the investigation's tool calls through budget.registerToolCall(by: id) like WorkerAgent does, or document explicitly that the investigation is intentionally a fixed-cost preamble exempt from the per-worker cap.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed. backgroundInvestigation builds its own ToolContext (lines 260-267) whose `charge` closure routes to budget.chargeTokens, then calls kbTool.call(...) (line 285) and search.call(...) (line 304) directly. Neither path invokes BudgetMeter.registerToolCall (AgentBudget.swift:75-83) — that per-worker tool-call ceiling is only applied for real workers at WorkerAgent.swift:156. So the investigation's tool calls are uncapped/uncounted against the per-worker tool-call budget; token budget IS honored via the charge closure. Benign today (≤2 calls) but an unguarded path if the investigation is ever extended. Accurate; P3 fits.  
- [x] **`deerflow-12`** — maxSteps derived from maxWorkers conflates parallel-fan-out budget with sequential step count  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:88, 461-470`  
  - **Category:** improvement · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** maxSteps = max(1, min(4, config.budget.maxWorkers)) reuses AgentBudget.maxWorkers (2 fast / 4 standard / 6 thorough, AgentBudget.swift:32-54), a value tuned for how many workers run IN PARALLEL in the native flow. In DeerFlow steps run SEQUENTIALLY and each adds latency + an observation-threaded prompt, so the right knob is 'how many sequential steps per plan iteration', which is a different cost model (wall-clock dominated, not parallelism-dominated). The hard min(...,4) cap also means thorough's maxWorkers=6 is clamped to 4 steps, so thorough never gets more steps-per-plan than standard — only more plan iterations.  
  - **Impact:** Step-count budgeting is borrowed from the wrong dimension: fast is fine, but thorough's extra step budget is silently ignored, and the sequential latency cost of each step is not reflected in how many are allowed. Suboptimal depth/cost tuning for the DeerFlow flow specifically.  
  - **Fix:** Introduce a DeerFlow-specific maxStepsPerPlan (e.g. derived from budget tier: 2/3/4 or 2/4/6) rather than reusing maxWorkers, and lift or re-justify the hard min(...,4) cap so thorough can plan deeper sequential chains.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed as an improvement. maxSteps = max(1, min(4, config.budget.maxWorkers)) (DeerFlowEngine.swift:88) reuses AgentBudget.maxWorkers (fast=2/standard=4/thorough=6, AgentBudget.swift:36/44/52), a value tuned for parallel fan-out in the native flow. In DeerFlow steps run sequentially (latency-dominated, each adding an observation-threaded prompt per deerflow-2), so step count is a different cost model than parallelism. The hard min(...,4) clamp means thorough's maxWorkers=6 yields only 4 steps/plan — identical to standard — so thorough gains depth only via more plan iterations (3 vs standard's 3; note both are clamped to 3 by min(maxRounds,3)). Valid conceptual improvement; P3 appropriate.  
- [x] **`deerflow-4`** — Re-plan loop wastes a planner LLM call on the final iteration whose steps never run  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:91-185`  
  - **Category:** bottleneck · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** adjusted  
  - **Problem:** On the last iteration (planIteration == maxPlanIterations), the engine still calls Self.plan (line 102), an expensive orchestrator round-trip, then executes its steps (lines 143-177). But because the loop terminates after this iteration, those final-iteration steps' findings are never reviewed by a planner and there is no opportunity to act on remaining gaps — they only reach the reporter. More importantly, when maxPlanIterations==3 and iteration 2 already produced hasEnoughContext:false, iteration 3 runs a fresh planner call AND a fresh step batch; this is the intended depth. However, if the planner on iteration 2 returns hasEnoughContext:true after its steps, the loop breaks — good. The waste case is subtler: there is no early check that skips the planner call when the prior iteration already executed steps and the wall-clock/token budget is nearly exhausted, so the engine can spend a planner round-trip and a step batch right up against the budget edge and then bust mid-step (line 146-149), discarding partial work.  
  - **Impact:** Extra orchestrator+worker token spend on a terminal iteration that cannot influence further planning; on tight budgets this can trip the wall-clock guard and abandon a half-finished final step, yielding a less complete report than if the run had stopped one iteration earlier.  
  - **Fix:** Add a pre-iteration budget headroom check: before calling plan() on a non-first iteration, estimate remaining wall-clock/token budget and break to the reporter if insufficient to complete a full plan+step batch. Optionally skip the planner call on the terminal iteration entirely if the goal is only to gather (re-plan adds no value when no further iteration follows).  
  - **Verify:** Partially real but overstated, and the headline ('wastes a planner LLM call on the final iteration whose steps never run') is wrong: the final iteration's steps DO run (lines 143-177) and their outputs reach the reporter via allOutputs — they are simply not re-reviewed by a planner, which is the intended terminal behavior. The genuine kernel is the absence of a 'budget nearly exhausted' early check: budget.checkWallClock() (AgentBudget.swift:101-107) only throws when ALREADY over cap, so the engine can spend a planner round-trip + step batch right at the budget edge and bust mid-step at line 146-149. But completed steps are preserved in allOutputs (line 171) and a busting worker still returns its gathered sources (WorkerAgent.swift:207-211), so 'discarding partial work' is inaccurate. This is a minor optimization opportunity, not a P2 bottleneck — downgrade to P3.  
- [x] **`deerflow-6`** — executedSubtasks accumulates steps even when a plan iteration declares enough context, inflating the emitted plan  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:115-139`  
  - **Category:** correctness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed  
  - **Problem:** newSubtasks is derived from dfPlan.steps and appended to executedSubtasks (line 122) BEFORE the hasEnoughContext check (line 134). If a planner iteration returns hasEnoughContext:true but ALSO returns non-empty steps (an LLM can do this despite the schema saying steps must be [] — the prompt only requests it, line 348/407), those steps are added to executedSubtasks and surface in the emitted ResearchPlan (line 132) and in the final plan handed to the reporter, yet they are never executed (the loop breaks at line 138). The emitted plan then advertises subtasks that produced no observations.  
  - **Impact:** The UI plan panel and the reporter's 'Plan strategy (N subtasks)' line (Synthesizer.swift:77) over-count actual work, mislabeling un-run steps as part of the executed plan. Minor, but it misrepresents what the engine actually did.  
  - **Fix:** Only append newSubtasks to executedSubtasks after confirming the steps will run (i.e. move the append below the hasEnoughContext/empty-steps short-circuit), or clear newSubtasks when hasEnoughContext is true before building the plan.  
  - **Verify:** Confirmed as described. newSubtasks (from dfPlan.steps) is appended to executedSubtasks at line 122 and the ResearchPlan built from executedSubtasks is emitted at line 132 — both BEFORE the hasEnoughContext check at line 134. If the planner returns hasEnoughContext:true with non-empty steps (schema only *requests* steps==[] in prose at line 348/407, not enforced in DFPlanWire decoding lines 366-377), those steps land in executedSubtasks, surface in the emitted plan and in finalPlan handed to the reporter (line 189-191), yet never execute because the loop breaks at line 138. Edge case requiring the LLM to contradict the prompt, and it only affects the displayed/handed plan (not observations), so P3 is correct.  
- [x] **`deerflow-7`** — Planner JSON decode silently discards partial output and over-relies on a brittle brace-scan  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:447-460, 489-499 (extractJSON)`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** M · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** adjusted  
  - **Problem:** extractJSON strips ``` fences then takes the substring between the first '{' and last '}' (lines 495-497). If the model emits prose containing stray braces (e.g. a step description with a JSON example, or LaTeX/code), the slice captures a malformed span and JSONDecoder fails, dropping to the single-direct-step fallback (lines 450-459). The fallback on a re-plan (isReplan==true) returns ZERO steps (line 455) AND hasEnoughContext:false, so the planning loop neither runs steps nor breaks on sufficiency — it falls through to the re-plan emit and loops again, potentially re-failing the same parse every iteration with no progress. Also, unlike ResearchPlanJSON.decode which surfaces a typed planningFailed error, this path only logs a warning, so a persistently malformed planner is invisible to the user.  
  - **Impact:** A planner model that wraps JSON in commentary or emits braces in step text can cause re-plan iterations that do no work (empty steps, not-enough-context), spinning until the iteration cap and then synthesizing from only the investigation. Diagnostics are buried in a log warning.  
  - **Fix:** Make extractJSON balance-aware (scan for the first '{' then match braces accounting for string literals), and on re-plan parse failure prefer to break the loop (treat as 'no further steps') rather than emit a not-enough-context plan with zero steps. Consider emitting a .warning event when the planner JSON fails so the user sees degraded planning.  
  - **Verify:** The central failure-mode claim is REFUTED but secondary observations hold, so net = adjusted/downgraded. extractJSON's first-{ to last-} brace scan (lines 489-499) is indeed brittle (shared verbatim by the native Planner.extractJSON). However the asserted infinite no-progress loop is wrong: on a re-plan parse failure the fallback returns steps:[] AND hasEnoughContext:false (lines 454-455), and the guard at line 134 is `if dfPlan.hasEnoughContext || newSubtasks.isEmpty` — newSubtasks.isEmpty is true, so the loop BREAKS at line 138, it does not loop and re-fail. On iteration 1 (isReplan==false) the fallback yields one direct step that executes. Also the 'native path surfaces a typed planningFailed error, this path only logs a warning' contrast is misleading: the native Planner catches ResearchPlanJSON.decode's planningFailed and silently falls back with a warning (Planner.swift:66-71), identical to DeerFlow. Remaining valid point is only the brace-scan fragility — minor robustness, P3.  
- [x] **`deerflow-8`** — Wall-clock bust mid-step-batch breaks the whole planning loop and skips later steps without a re-plan signal  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:144-150`  
  - **Category:** robustness · **Effort:** S · **Confidence:** medium · **Verdict:** adjusted  
  - **Problem:** Inside the per-step loop, a wall-clock bust emits a warning and `break planning` (line 149), abandoning all remaining steps in the current batch AND all remaining plan iterations. That is reasonable for stopping, but it does so without emitting a reflectionConcluded(continuing:false) the way the top-of-loop budget guard does (lines 95-98). The UI's reflection state may be left mid-flight (a prior reflectionStarted without a matching concluded) on the path where a previous iteration emitted reflectionStarted at line 182.  
  - **Impact:** Minor UI/state inconsistency: the activity feed can show an in-progress reflection that never concludes when the run is cut short by wall-clock during a step batch. No data loss (gathered outputs still reach the reporter), but the progress UI can look stuck.  
  - **Fix:** Before `break planning` at line 149, emit `.reflectionConcluded(continuing: false, remainingGaps: 0)` to balance any outstanding reflectionStarted, mirroring the top-of-loop guard.  
  - **Verify:** The structural observation is true but the stated user-visible symptom is REFUTED. True part: a mid-step wall-clock bust emits only `.warning(...)` and `break planning` (lines 144-150), abandoning remaining steps + iterations without the `.reflectionConcluded(continuing:false)` that the top-of-loop guard emits (lines 95-98). Refuted part: the claimed 'prior reflectionStarted without a matching concluded leaves the UI mid-flight' cannot happen — reflectionStarted (line 182) and reflectionConcluded(continuing:true) (line 183) are emitted as an unconditional adjacent pair with nothing between them, and the current iteration hasn't reached line 182 at the time of the mid-step bust. The UI status at bust time is `.working` (last worker) and is then overwritten by synthesis events (AppEnvironment.swift:584-595 + synthesis), so no stuck `.reflecting` state. Cosmetic/correctness-of-events nit at most; severity stays P3.  
- [ ] **`deerflow-9`** — Duplicated engine scaffolding between DeerFlowEngine and ResearchEngine invites drift  
  - **Where:** `Swift Deep Research/Engine/DeerFlowEngine.swift:43-66, 199-215; Swift Deep Research/Engine/ResearchEngine.swift:47-62, 218-234`  
  - **Category:** architecture · **Effort:** L · **Confidence:** high · **Verdict:** confirmed
